Chunks stay the unit of mapping and of reuse.

Design it twice on the protection rule:

| | (A) One mapping, flipped RW around each publish | **(B) Chosen: two views of the same pages** | (C) One mapping, written only before it is ever executable |
|---|---|---|---|
| macOS, `MAP_JIT` | sound: `pthread_jit_write_protect_np` is per thread | the same call; the two "views" are one address and the per-thread toggle, so the shape is one | sound |
| Linux, `mprotect` | unsound: protection is per mapping, so for the length of a compile the chunk is RW for every thread, including one executing a trace in it | sound: the execute view is `PROT_READ | PROT_EXEC` from `new` and is never remapped; the write view is `PROT_READ | PROT_WRITE`, is held only by the compile lock's owner, and is never executed | sound |
| Tail of a chunk | usable | usable | wasted once the chunk is sealed |
| Mappings | one | two | one |
| What the test can show | nothing: `HeapPages` has no protection | thread A loops in a trace in chunk 7 while thread B publishes into chunk 7, natively, on both targets | |

(B) is chosen. On Linux the arena is one `memfd_create` file of `capacity` bytes, created with `MFD_ALLOW_SEALING` and sealed against shrinking and growing, mapped twice as a whole with `MAP_SHARED`, once RW and once RX, and chunks are carved inside the region. On macOS it is one `MAP_JIT` anonymous mapping, the one-region rule of §B.8, and the per-thread toggle around each write, which is the same shape with the two views at one address; there is no process-wide protection change after `new` on either platform. On macOS x86-64 the system has no per-thread write protection, so `code` reads `pthread_jit_write_protect_supported_np` once, as php's `zend_jit_startup` reads it into `zend_write_protect` (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit.c:3819`), and toggles only where it answers 1: on Intel the `MAP_JIT` mapping stays readable, writable and executable on every thread as `mmap` made it. php's ZTS build has its buffer so only between `zend_jit_unprotect` and `zend_jit_protect` (`:3553-3600`), whose `mprotect` back to read and execute is the process-wide change this section refuses; Linux's write view stands writable the same way. The cache maintenance is nothing, php's `ir_mem_flush` calling `sys_icache_invalidate` on Apple aarch64 alone and `__builtin___clear_cache`, which emits nothing on x86-64 (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/ir/ir.c:1962-1974`). `MAP_JIT` stays on x86-64 because a hardened runtime refuses a writable and executable mapping without it, even with the `com.apple.security.cs.allow-jit` entitlement, and a build without the hardened runtime maps it, and a plain writable and executable mapping, alike (#3524). `HeapPages`, the test adapter, is one allocation of `UnsafeCell<u8>` handing out two raw pointers to it; it tests the arena's bookkeeping, which is all a heap adapter can. The internal `Pages` seam is `map_pair(len)`, one span and its two views, `write(at, bytes)` through the write view, and `sync(range)` and `rx(at)` on the execute view's addresses, with the same two adapters (revision 19). Cache maintenance, stated once: on x86-64 nothing; on aarch64 the data cache is physically tagged, so after the write through the write view one sequence on the execute view's addresses suffices, `dc cvau` per line, `dsb ish`, `ic ivau` per line, `dsb ish`, `isb`, done on Linux by an inline-assembly routine in `code` that reads the line sizes from `CTR_EL0` (#2943), and on macOS by libSystem's `sys_icache_invalidate`, which `code` calls over the bytes written. Whether a thread that later enters a reused chunk needs its own `isb`: on macOS aarch64, #2741's test entered a body on one thread per core and dropped it, published a different body at the same address of the reused chunk, and had a thread that had never entered the chunk, waiting on an atomic pointer with no system call before its entry, enter it; that thread read the new body in every round, with its own `isb` and without, so `code` asks none of it on macOS. The test cannot choose which core the entering thread runs on, so a stale fetch it did not meet is not one it excluded. On Linux aarch64, with two aliases, #2943 ran the same test in a Linux container on Apple silicon, the sequence above issued over the execute view's addresses after the write through the other alias: the entering thread read the new body in every round of every run, with its own `isb` and without, so `code` asks none of it on Linux either. Those are the cores the macOS test ran on, whose `CTR_EL0` reports an instruction cache not coherent with the data cache (`DIC` clear), so the container answers for that hardware under two aliases and not for another vendor's cores; the Linux runner (#2296) runs the test on its own, and until it has, the answer on Linux aarch64 is the container's. On macOS `code` reaches the mapping and the toggle through `libc`, already in the lock; on Linux it reaches the `memfd`, its seals and the two mappings through `rustix`, as #2945 ruled, and closes the descriptor once both views stand. Which targets have an adapter is named once, by `php_jit`'s `build.rs`. The three lifetime rules are carried by types inside `table`: publication under the compile lock, no write after publication, code outlives every call. **No byte is patched** (#3229's ruling, revision 81). php links a hot exit to its side trace by patching the exit's jump once the side is compiled (`zend_jit_link_side_trace` → `ir_patch`, `Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_ir.c:17413`), which writes code another core may be running, the hazard (A) is rejected for. Each exit has a door in the request instead (§4.6), which the request's first crossing of the exit into its side opens, and no published trace or table word is written after its publish. `Full::Code` is php's `COMPILER_ERROR`: the emit found no room in the arena, and the recording aborts as a recoverable stop that counts one attempt toward the word's blacklist (`zend_jit_trace.c:7612-7622`, `:8452-8461`); `Full::Traces` is php's `TOO_MANY_TRACES` (#3015). The research note did not show that the Linux mapping and sealing calls behave as described, §B.8 covering macOS only; #2943's container run shows they do: `/proc/self/maps` holds the write view `rw-s` and the execute view `r-xs`, both of the one `memfd`, and the file refuses to grow or shrink.

**A fork's child takes its own arena** (#2943). A shared mapping is shared with a forked child too, while what says where code goes, the chunks' free flags, the cursor and the compile lock, is private memory a fork copies; so on Linux, where the two views map one `memfd` shared, a child and its parent would publish at the same offset of the same chunk and each run what the other wrote. php keeps its allocation pointer inside its one buffer, under `zend_shared_alloc_lock` (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit.c:3824`, `:3135`), because processes sharing one opcache segment share one buffer; this engine shares an arena among one process's threads (design §3.4), and a forked child is a separate process with its own copy of the arena's bookkeeping, so the child gets its own arena at the same addresses instead. The engine's fork holds each table, its compile lock taken, once every engine thread is joined (`tier::Inherited`); before the fork the chunks in use are written into a second sealed `memfd`, free chunks left as zero pages, and in the child that file is mapped with `MAP_FIXED` over the execute view and then the write view, so every trace's entrance and every address into code stays valid and the child writes only its own pages. The parent pays the copy at each fork, for the chunks in use alone. Where the copy or the mapping is refused, the child's table keeps no arena and publishes no more code, which leaves its code valid, since the parent writes only where chunks were free at the fork; the one refusal not covered is a kernel that unmaps the execute view's range before refusing its replacement. This keeps the rule above, no write after publication, across a fork, where it said nothing of one. On macOS the `MAP_PRIVATE` mapping is copied on write and the child takes nothing.

A stricter posture, in which the write view exists only between `publish`'s first and last store, is a `Pages` policy behind the same seam and is not the default: it costs two syscalls per publish and defends against an attacker who already has an arbitrary write, which is not a threat model the design claims. php leaves its whole buffer RWX under `PERF_DUMP` and `GDB` (`research/data/jit-research/php-src-jit-startup-protect.txt:46,121`); the dual map makes that unnecessary here. `MapJit` is a startup value from the policy.

Tested at the seam under Miri with `HeapPages`: chunk fitting, alignment, live counts, and that a `Code` dropped last frees its chunk. Natively, on both targets: `mov eax, 7; ret` on x86-64 and `mov w0, #7; ret` on aarch64 through the two views; the concurrent test above, a spinning trace in one chunk on one thread and a hundred publishes into the same chunk on another, asserting the trace's result on every iteration; and the reused-chunk test. On macOS aarch64 all three pass (#2741): `mov w0, #7; ret` returns 7, and the spinning body answered 7 on every one of its calls while the hundred landed and were entered. In Linux containers (#2943) all three pass natively on aarch64, and the first two on x86-64 under emulation, which is evidence and not a reading; the Linux runner (#2296) gives the readings. On macOS x86-64 (#3524) the first two pass under Rosetta 2 on Apple silicon, with the forked table test, which is evidence and not a reading: there a probe read `pthread_jit_write_protect_supported_np` as 0 and ran `mov eax, 7; ret` from a `MAP_JIT` mapping written without the toggle, with and without the hardened runtime, where a plain writable and executable mapping was refused under it; no Intel runner has given the readings.

### 4.5 `table`: the owner of every published trace

```rust
#[derive(Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)] pub(crate) struct TraceId(u32);   // one space; < 2^24, the word's width

pub(crate) enum Kind { Trace /* , Region: month three, the same Entry ABI */ }
pub(crate) struct Trace {
    code: Code, id: TraceId, kind: Kind, start: Start, root: TraceId, exits: Box<[Exit]>, map_ptrs: Box<[MapPtrRaw]>,
    marks: Box<[Mark]>, leaves: Option<ExitId>, fuzzed: Fuzzed,   // revision 36
    links: Box<[TraceId]>,            // every trace an exit links to; an id is never reused within an epoch
}
pub(crate) struct Exit { opline: Option<OplineIdx>, landing: Landing, link: Option<TraceId>, word: Word }   // opline: revision 26
pub(crate) enum Full { Traces, Children, Exits, Code }          // php's TOO_MANY_TRACES, _CHILDREN, _EXITS; a full arena, php's COMPILER_ERROR (revision 31)
pub(crate) enum Unpublished { Full(Full), Malformed(&'static str) }   // Malformed: a Compiled that does not hold together

pub(crate) struct TraceTable { /* one per epoch; read side: segments of slots, each written once under the lock and read without it;
                                  write side: compile: Mutex<(code::Arena, Counts, Segments)>, taken with PoisonError::into_inner */ }
impl TraceTable {
    pub(crate) fn new(policy: &Policy) -> TraceTable;   // an arena that cannot be mapped is none: a publish numbers its trace and keeps no code (revisions 27, 34)
    /// Takes the compile lock, copies the bytes into the arena, builds one `Exit` per `ExitSpec`
    /// with a zero `Word`, resolves each link to its `TraceId`,
    /// assigns the next id, writes the `PERF` line and `PERF_DUMP` record if `jit_debug` asks, and
    /// publishes with a release store. Transactional: the arena cursor and `Counts` advance only
    /// after the copy, so a panic inside leaves the table as it was. `Full` is php's
    /// `TOO_MANY_TRACES`, `TOO_MANY_CHILDREN` or `TOO_MANY_EXITS` by which bound was hit, or `Code`, php's
    /// `COMPILER_ERROR`, where the arena had no room for this body, which fills nothing (revision 31).
    pub(crate) fn publish(&self, compiled: Compiled, start: Start) -> Result<TraceId, Unpublished>;
    /// Whether the next id reached `opcache.jit_max_root_traces`, and nothing else (revision 31); the hot root that
    /// finds it so is refused with `TOO_MANY_TRACES` and calls `stop`, php's `zend_jit_stop_counter_handlers`, after
    /// which `stopped` holds and dispatch claims no word of the epoch (revision 27).
    pub(crate) fn full(&self) -> bool;
    pub(crate) fn stop(&self);
    pub(crate) fn stopped(&self) -> bool;
    /// Lock-free: two loads, the second acquire. A slot is written once, so the borrow is the
    /// table's. `None` in the window §3.2 names, which means "not compiled" to the caller.
    pub(crate) fn get(&self, id: TraceId) -> Option<&Trace>;
}
/// The table of the epoch `req` started in, kept by `tier::epoch_state` (revision 27).
pub(crate) fn of<'r>(req: &'r Request, policy: &Policy) -> Option<&'r TraceTable>;
```

The id space is one. A root and a side trace are numbered from the same counter; `Trace.root` names the root a side belongs to (itself, for a root), and `Counts` keeps php's three bounds, `opcache.jit_max_root_traces`, `opcache.jit_max_side_traces` per root, and `opcache.jit_max_exit_counters`, as numbers, not as widths. The read side is a segmented array, a fixed table of segment pointers each holding 1,024 slots, allocated on demand under the compile lock and read with two loads. A slot is written once and never cleared within its epoch, so `get` borrows the table, and entering a trace clones nothing and changes no count. A `Func` word's 24 bits and an `Exit` word's 24 bits hold the same kind of number, and `get` is the only reader of either. **`Kind` is the answer to the tier bit question** (`research/llvm-research.r1.md` D.9): a month-three region over hot traces is a table entry with the same `Entry` ABI, entered from the same word by the same id, and the word's layout does not change for it.

The lifetime rule. A trace's code embeds addresses from the units it inlined and the published classes it read, and all of them live as long as the epoch (design §5.14 *Identities*, §6.3). So the trace table belongs to the epoch: the epoch's arena keeps it in the one slot the tier face gives the tier (`tier::epoch_state`, `Epoch::tier_state`), made by the epoch's first request that asks and dropped with the arena, so `php_engine` learns nothing of what it is (revision 27). A request reaches only the table of the epoch it holds, and a restart starts the next epoch with an empty table, as `zend_jit_restart` resets php's buffer. The old table and its code are freed with the old epoch, once no request, job or claim holds it. No trace is freed alone. When `opcache.jit_max_root_traces` is reached, the table stops the hot counters and compiles nothing more until the restart (`:8164-8167`). A full code arena stops nothing: each compile that finds no room aborts as `COMPILER_ERROR`, as php's does (#3015).

Observability. php's `opcache.jit_debug` bits `PERF` (0x10) and `PERF_DUMP` (0x20) (`research/data/jit-research/php-src-jit-header.txt:59-60`) are honoured with php's outputs: under either bit a line per trace in `/tmp/perf-<pid>.map`, `<start> <size> TRACE-<n>$<where>$<line>` (`ir_perf_map_register`, `zend_jit_trace_name`), and under `PERF_DUMP`, on Linux alone, where php's `ir_perf_jitdump_open` finds an ELF executable, a `JIT_CODE_LOAD` record with the name and the code bytes in `/tmp/jit-<pid>.dump` (`ir_perf_jitdump_register`); php writes no line entries, so the `marks` are not written there and stay the disassembly's. php opens each file once per process, truncating it; here the engine's start truncates both, writes the jitdump's header and maps it once, the event `perf inject` finds it by, each epoch's table appends one write per line or record, numbering its records from the engine's one `code_id` as php's one static does, and the engine's end writes php's `JIT_CODE_CLOSE` record (revision 27). Both are written by `publish` under the compile lock. Nothing is written when the bits are clear.

Tested at the seam under Miri with `HeapPages`: publish, get, ids across a segment boundary, `Full` for each of the three bounds, a full table that stops compiling, a new epoch's table that starts empty, a publish that panics and leaves `Counts` unchanged, and that a dropped trace returns its chunk. Natively, `mov eax, 7; ret` (`mov w0, #7; ret` on aarch64) runs through `publish` and `get(..).code.entry()`. The `PERF` line is diffed against php's for one trace of one witness.

### 4.6 `enter`: the crossing

```rust
pub(crate) enum Left { Exit(Exited), Unwound(FrameRef), Interrupted(FrameRef) }   // an unwind hands back the frame that stands, nothing landed (revision 45); the stub the frame stood at the loop's header (revision 51)
pub(crate) struct Exited { exit: ExitId, frame: FrameRef }
/// Call the code with the request's trace number the trace's for the call, resume a panic a
/// wrapper caught, return a bailout a wrapper caught, and otherwise land the exit: read the current
/// frame from the VM stack, set its `frame.opline`, and release the temporaries a landing's
/// `frees` names; a throwable a kernel left pending is not landed (revision 32).
/// Reads no word and follows no link: what happens next is `dispatch`'s.
pub(crate) fn enter(req: &mut Request, frame: FrameRef, trace: &Trace) -> Result<Left, Bailout>;

/// The first argument of every trace and every kernel wrapper. A local of `enter`. Compiled code
/// reads `req` at offset zero and `interrupt` at offset eight and nothing else; `pending` has no C
/// layout and is the wrappers' alone. It borrows the request for its life; generic over the request
/// so that `enter`'s own tests and the bench cross it without an engine (revision 20).
#[repr(C)] pub(crate) struct ExitCtx<'a, R = Request> { req: *mut R, interrupt: *const AtomicBool, pending: Option<Pending>, lent: PhantomData<&'a mut R> }
pub(crate) enum Pending { Bailout(Bailout), Panic(Box<dyn Any + Send>) }

#[repr(u32)] pub(crate) enum Status { Continue = 0, Exception, Bailout, Panic }   // produced by wrappers alone, branched on by compiled code

kernels! {                                   // lower::Kernel's five (revisions 28, 32, 65)
    Request => mod wrapped {
        Step => step(func: u32) -> Stood = tier::step_held;     // the opline of the op array numbered func served whole (§5.2)
        PushCall => push_call(func: u32, callee: u32) -> Stood = tier::step_held;   // an inline INIT_* where its cache slot holds callee
        Call => call(func: u32, callee: u32) -> Stood = tier::call_held;   // a DO_*CALL into the callee the trace follows, no dispatch
        Release => release(word: u64, type_info: u32) = tier::release;   // zval_ptr_dtor
        Copy => copy(word: u64, type_info: u32) -> Zval;       // ZVAL_COPY_DEREF
    }
}
```

Each `kernels!` line expands to `pub(crate) unsafe extern "C" fn <name>(cx: *mut ExitCtx, frame: *mut u8, opline: u32, ...args, out: *mut T) -> Status`, with no `out` for a line with no `-> T`, in a `mod` the invocation names, and one entry in the `Symbols` table, `symbols()`, whose match over `lower::Kernel` holds the list to every kernel `lower` calls: the wrapper reconstitutes `&mut Request` from `cx.req`, hands the kernel the frame's room and the opline's index, calls it inside `catch_unwind(AssertUnwindSafe(..))`, writes its value through `out` (a `Zval` as `ZVAL_COPY_VALUE` writes it, never the slot's `u2`), and maps the result to a `Status`, storing a `Bailout` or a panic payload into `cx.pending` and answering `Exception` where the kernel returned with a throwable pending. A kernel that reads its frame finds it by its room (`tier::at_room`) and stands it at the opline, php's `SAVE_OPLINE()` (`tier::stand`), so what it raises is reported there. `= tier::item` names the engine item the kernel serves through: the wrappers' `mod` imports it, so its removal fails the build, and `api-check` writes it into `tier.lock`'s kernels section (§5.3). `interrupt` is `req.limits().interrupt()`, the flag the engine's timer and signal delivery set (design §5.8), taken by `enter` once so that compiled code needs no layout knowledge of `Request`. The wrappers are `extern "C"`, not `"C-unwind"`, because every panic is caught before it reaches compiled code (`research/llvm-research.r1.md` B.4).

The touch (revision 97, #3346; #2957's note; revision 98, #3421). On a request's first entry of a trace whose code reads runtime-cache slots, `enter` touches each of `Trace.map_ptrs` (`tier::touch`), which answers the slot's address, a `tier::CachedSlot`, where the request has laid the op array's cache down, and lays nothing: php lays a function's cache at the first `INIT_*` that pushes a call of it and at the first lookup of it by name (`init_func_run_time_cache`, `zend_fetch_function`), and so does the engine, so a trace entered before its inlined callee's first call reads a tag that is no place's at that slot, the opline its handler's, until the call lays the cache, and each later entry touches again the slots it left unanswered that a call can lay. `enter` keeps the addresses in the request, past the headers of the trace's calls in the one list its body takes the address of as it enters (`enter::Images`), so a jump carries them as it carries the headers and a trace that reads none pays nothing for them; only the slots compiled code reads are `Trace.map_ptrs`. A function's cache stands where it was laid for as long as the request runs, so the code reads the slot there; a file's own code, whose every activation lays a fresh cache, is answered none, and its slot reads a tag that is no place's.

**A frame served from outside the loop** (revision 99, #3420). A trace that ends at the `RETURN` of a frame `zend_call_function` served, a native's callback or a magic accessor a handler reached (`exec::Caller::Outside` or `Caller::Loop`, 9183b7a9c), ends the serving in compiled code where the frame is the serving's entry, as php's `zend_jit_leave_func` returns `ZEND_VM_RETURN` to `execute_ex` for a `ZEND_CALL_TOP` frame: the `leave` kernel hands the value over with its owner (`tier::return_entry`, the interpreter's own entry return: the observer told, the serving's return value set), compiled code answers `lower::RETURNED`, `enter` hands back `Left::Returned`, `dispatch` answers `Flow::Returned`, and `execute` returns at once on `Stepped::Done`, so the interpreter serves none of the frame's oplines. Any other such frame leaves through the trace's end exit, as before. A frame such a trace returns past and goes on below, whose `RETURN` is not its last op, is held by the recorder's entry-frame rule (§4.1) only in the serving it was recorded in, so where the trace runs in another it may be that serving's entry: `p`'s return trace, recorded under a loop, runs past the outermost `p` that `array_map` calls (#3689). Where the step of such an opline leaves the serving's entry frame, the `step` kernel answers that it stands nowhere (`Stood::NOWHERE`) and compiled code past the step of an opline that leaves a frame the trace did not push, php's `may_be_top_frame`, answers `lower::RETURNED` (`Emit::halts`), as php's trace guards the IP that `zend_jit_leave_func_helper` or the handler of a `RETURN` answers for a `ZEND_CALL_TOP` frame and takes `jit_stub_trace_halt` (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_ir.c:11095-11112`, `:17218-17228`). The `__get`, `__set` and `__isset` a handler serves through an `execute` of their own (§4.3's *Objects*) are such frames, and so is `array_map`'s callback; the handler that reached them is still served through `handle`, and nothing a trace keeps is a fact their code can change (§4.2, revision 83).

**What an entry looks up** (revision 100, #3437). php enters a trace through the handler its op array holds and looks nothing up on the way; an entry here reads the site's word and its trace's slot in the table, checks the type of its request's tier state once per visit (`tier::state`, since the engine hands a tier its state through the request alone), and looks up nothing else that does not change; what it still lays per entry is the `ExitCtx` and its hold's two words (`enter::Holding`). The request lends each visit its tier's state, its hold on the epoch it started in and that epoch's trace table (`enter::lent`), the hold taken at the request's first visit and the table kept from the first visit that finds it made, so an entry takes no `Arc` count of the epoch or of the engine's `Shared`, which the engine's threads share, and finds the table through no type check; the lend stands on the request laying its tier's state once and lending it only shared, which php_engine's types hold outside `api::request`, where the request is made (`Request::tier_state`, `tier::TierState`), and which `enter::lent` asserts past each lend under `debug_assertions`. An entry after the one that settled its trace's row, every call in place made and every slot the touch answers answered, reads that row alone (`enter::Rows::settled`), where an entry before it lays the row out as the touch above says. What it writes is the request's alone: the hold and the kept table, each laid once, and a row's `settled` flag, false at each entry before the row settles and true from then on; no published trace, table slot or word is written, and the published image stays immutable and `Sync` (ADR-0006).

Where `pending` lives. An `ExitCtx` is a local of the `enter` that made it, compiled code holds its address for exactly the duration of that call, and a nested `enter` has its own. The nested case, walked: an inner wrapper stores its kernel's panic in the inner context; the inner `enter` resumes it; the unwind crosses the nested `execute` and the outer kernel; the outer wrapper's `catch_unwind` catches it into the outer context; the outer `enter` resumes it; one panic, the inner payload, reaches the request boundary. A bailout in the inner trace is the inner `enter`'s `Err`, the nested `execute`'s `Err`, the outer kernel's `Bailout`, which every kernel propagates from a nested `execute` as it does from any callee, and the outer wrapper's `BAILOUT`. The request state the crossing touches is monotone: the addresses and rows it lays per trace, a row settled once, and the hold and the table the request lends (revision 100).

The raw-pointer invariant, restated per fiber stack. `&mut Request` crosses as a raw pointer under the interpreter's own invariant: on one fiber stack exactly one frame chain executes on this request, and compiled code returns before anything else on that stack touches the request. A kernel called from a trace may suspend the fiber, through a native that calls `Fiber::suspend`; the trace's machine frame then waits on the fiber's stack while another fiber's code, interpreted or compiled, touches the request, exactly as an interpreter handler's frame waits across the same switch. Nothing the trace holds is invalidated by that: its frame is on the fiber's own VM stack pages (design §5.11), its `ExitCtx` is a local on the fiber's own machine stack, and the register-resident scalars it holds belong to slots only its own frame can name. What the switch does invalidate, a memory-derived fact about a shared array or object, is what the clobber rule (§4.2) already reloads after any call-effect kernel, and a suspend is inside such a kernel. So the invariant is per stack, and the design writes it that way.

**The entry** (revision 81; revision 142). A trace's body takes, as it enters, the entry state as the parameters of its tail-convention body:

- the room it was handed;
- one word, saying where it is entered, the frame's place on the stack with it, and whether its calls' headers in the request are ready;
- the address of its addresses in the request;
- where the frame stands as a kernel would say it (`Stood`).

`enter` passes them as it calls the trace's prologue with the body's address. A jump from another trace passes the same in registers, from where the exit or the link leaves the frame: it stands the frame at the exit's opline as `enter`'s landing would and makes a tail call to the body, so a trace is entered alike whichever way it is reached.

A jump reads one door of the request's: three words per exit, link and escape of the trace, past its calls' headers in the request's addresses, naming the body and the addresses of the trace the door leads to. `enter` writes them once, when the request enters that trace through `enter` with its row ready, so a trace is jumped to only once the request has entered it through `enter`. A closed door answers the exit, as today.

A trace's every answer writes its own number at `lower::TRACE_AT`.

An exit jumps only where the entry can be laid in code: it names its opline, or, a link's, it names none and the frame stands where the linked trace opens; its landing frees nothing; and the code runs where it leaves in a frame of that op array, the frame the jump enters on, which in a recursion, whose frames leave through the exits of the frame their header serves, is not always the frame the exit names. `enter` reads the answer against the trace `TRACE_AT` names, the one entered or one it jumped to, and returns it beside the answer (`enter::Answered`), so the exit landed is the exit of the trace that took it. The request's trace number, php's `EG(jit_trace_num)`, is the entered trace's for the crossing; nothing reads it inside a jump.

`enter` does what no exit block can and nothing else. It does not read the exit's word, does not follow a link, and does not enter a side trace: it returns `Exited` and `dispatch` decides (§4.9). `CHECK_EXCEPTION` remains the `Resume` contract, and `INVALIDATE` still does not exist. `PACKED_GUARD` is a flag on the exit a layout guard takes, as php's is, and no side trace's entry type: a side enters with the types its parent's exit knew, an array's without its layout, and holds the layout at its first read by a long key, as php's holds `MAY_BE_PACKED_GUARD`.

Tested at the seam: a hand-assembled body calling `wrapped::step` through an `ExitCtx`; a panicking test kernel resumed at the request boundary; every wrapper under `force`; `jit.mode=exit-fuzz` (G5), as the next paragraph states it; a kernel that re-enters PHP into a compiled root whose kernel panics, asserting one panic at the request boundary with the inner payload; `set_time_limit(1); for (;;) {}` under `force`, whose fatal must be php's to the byte; a `pcntl_async_signals(true)` handler delivered into a compiled loop; and a fiber that suspends from inside a kernel called by a trace and is resumed from another compiled loop.

As ruled on #3055 (revision 40): Under `jit.mode=exit-fuzz` every guard whose exit is right whatever its value holds fails once per process, the interrupt guard included; the interpreter continues at the landing, and the run must match the oracle byte for byte. A jump's direction guard and `push_call`'s refusal are not failed: their exits are right only where their condition holds (#3055).

### 4.7 `policy`, pure

```rust
pub(crate) struct Policy { mode: Mode, compile: Compile, hot_loop: u32, hot_func: u32, hot_return: u32, hot_side_exit: u32,
    blacklist_root: u8, blacklist_side: u8, max_trace_length: u32, max_root_traces: u32, max_side_traces: u32,
    max_exit_counters: u32, max_loop_unrolls: u8, max_recursive_calls: u8, max_recursive_returns: u8,
    max_polymorphic_calls: u8, buffer_size: usize, debug: Debug, map_jit: MapJit }
pub(crate) enum Mode { Off, Tracing, Force, ExitFuzz }
pub(crate) enum Compile { Thread, Inline }                                     // jit.compile; Force and ExitFuzz imply Inline (revision 34), and a request's jit_debug that asks for a root's end line compiles its recording inline (revision 106)
pub(crate) enum State { Cold, Recording, Compiled(TraceId), Blacklisted }
pub(crate) enum Outcome { Published(TraceId), Abort(Stop), Blacklist(Stop) }   // Abort counts an attempt; Blacklist does not wait for the count
pub(crate) enum Spent { After(u8), At(u32) }   // a root's attempts, php's bad root cache; an exit's heat, php's exit counter, at hot_side_exit + blacklist_side_trace (revision 56)

/// Where a claimed word lives. Owned, so a claim can leave the thread that made it.
pub(crate) enum Holder { Root { epoch: Epoch, func: FuncRef, at: SafepointIdx }, Exit { epoch: Epoch, trace: TraceId, exit: ExitId } }   // Epoch: a hold on the epoch (design §5.14)
impl Holder { pub(crate) fn word(&self) -> &Word; }

impl Policy {
    pub(crate) fn from_ini(ini: &StartupIni) -> Result<Policy, IniError>;
    #[inline(always)] pub(crate) fn mode(&self) -> Mode;
    #[inline(always)] pub(crate) fn threshold(&self, start: Start) -> u32;
    pub(crate) fn spent(&self, start: Start) -> Spent;                         // revision 56
}
#[inline(always)] pub(crate) fn read(word: &Word) -> State;                 // relaxed; all ones is Blacklisted
#[inline(always)] pub(crate) fn bump(word: &Word) -> u32;                   // racy: load, saturating add, store; returns the new heat
/// CAS cold → recording on `holder.word()`. `None` if another thread won. Dropped without `settle`,
/// the claim settles as `Abort(Stop::CompilerError)`. `Send`: it holds the epoch, not borrows.
pub(crate) fn claim(holder: Holder, policy: &Policy, start: Start) -> Option<Claim>;
#[must_use] pub(crate) struct Claim { /* holder, start, attempts limit; Drop settles */ }
impl Claim { pub(crate) fn start(&self) -> Start; }
/// One release store. `Abort` also stores zero to the heat half, as php's counter resets when it fires (§A.3).
pub(crate) fn settle(claim: Claim, outcome: &Outcome);
pub(crate) static INI: &[IniEntry];
pub(crate) fn dump(sink: &mut dyn Write, event: &TraceEvent);               // TRACE n start/stop/abort/blacklisted, php's words
```

Nothing here touches a request, a frame, a lock or a table. A `Claim` owns its word's holder instead of borrowing the word: a claim made on a request thread is settled on the compiler's thread (§4.8), after the request that made it may have ended, and the epoch its holder holds keeps the word alive for at least as long as the claim does. Why a claim is a value and not a boolean: the word is engine-shared, and if the thread that won the compare-and-swap leaves without a release store, because a kernel panicked under `step` or a `Bailout` came out of recording, the root stays `recording` for every request that follows, never compiled, never blacklisted, and silent. A `Claim`'s `Drop` is the store a panic would otherwise skip; the stop it records is php's `COMPILER_ERROR` (§A.4), and it counts an attempt, so a root that panics sixteen times is blacklisted.

Directives keep php's names and defaults so an oracle configuration replays unchanged: `opcache.jit` (php's CRTO number or word; `function` and any trigger other than `HOT_TRACE` are accepted, logged once at startup, and run as tracing, because there is one tier and no optimizer, §C.2 item 4; `disable` is `Mode::Off`; `off` and `0` with a buffer trace only what a request publishes after turning the tier on), `opcache.jit_buffer_size` 64M (zero is `Mode::Off`), `opcache.jit_hot_loop` 61, `opcache.jit_hot_func` 127, `opcache.jit_hot_return` 8, `opcache.jit_hot_side_exit` 8, `opcache.jit_blacklist_root_trace` 16, `opcache.jit_blacklist_side_trace` 8, `opcache.jit_max_root_traces` 1024, `opcache.jit_max_side_traces` 128, `opcache.jit_max_exit_counters` 8192, `opcache.jit_max_loop_unrolls` 8, `opcache.jit_max_recursive_calls` 2, `opcache.jit_max_recursive_returns` 2, `opcache.jit_max_polymorphic_calls` 2, `opcache.jit_max_trace_length` 1024, `opcache.jit_debug` (php's bit names). Two engine-only directives: `jit.mode` (`tracing`, `force`, `exit-fuzz`) and `jit.compile` (`thread`, `inline`). `jit.mode` names the mode over `opcache.jit=off`, `tracing` and directives that set none, and not where the directives set say php's tier never starts, `opcache.jit=disable`, a setting php refuses or a zero `opcache.jit_buffer_size`: that program is about having no tier, so the interpreter serves it, and the lane writes no line (#3136's ruling, revision 58). Both are accepted from any host, because a startup ini has no way to know its host and php's own `opcache.jit_debug` is likewise available everywhere; `force` and `exit-fuzz` log one line at startup, and `inline` is a legitimate production choice for a short-lived CLI process. The two engine-only directives are system ini, and so are php's but `opcache.jit` and `opcache.jit_debug`: engine state is immutable after `Engine::new` (design §4.4). `ini_set('opcache.jit', ...)` at run time is honoured as php honours it: php reads `JIT_G(on)` only as `zend_persist` persists an op array, giving it its counters while the tier is on (`Z/ext/opcache/zend_persist.c:721-726`, `:1471-1473`), and no counter reads it again. So the engine asks the tier once per unit as its cache holds it how its words are laid (`Tier::laid`), and `Policy::laid` reads `JIT_G(on)` of the publishing request: `on`, or a `jit.mode` lane, makes the unit's words count, and anything else seals them, so no visit acts on them. `JIT_G` is the request's, one `TierStatus` it holds (`UnitCache::tier_setting`, read by the tier through `tier::setting`). Each request starts from its engine's start: what `opcache.jit`'s handler folded at startup, which the tier rewrites at bind as php's `zend_jit_startup` and `zend_jit_check_support` do (`Tier::setting`). The opcache extension's `on_jit`, php's `OnUpdateJit`, alone writes it, folding each `ini_set('opcache.jit', ...)` as `zend_jit_config` does: `off` keeps the digits the last setting that turned the tier on wrote, and a refused setting leaves the tier neither enabled nor on, after which every change is refused with php's warning, so what the request includes after is never traced. Counting words start cold where the tier was on at the start, since php's `zend_jit_activate` fills its pool of hot counters at every request start where `JIT_G(on)` holds then (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit.c:3906-3912`). Where it was set up and off nothing fills that pool, which starts at zero (`zend_jit.c:78`), so a counter php hands an op array persisted after the switch fires at its first arrival, `0 - cost <= 0`, and starts again at `ZEND_JIT_COUNTER_INIT` (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_vm_helpers.c:470`): such a unit's words are laid primed, their heat at the top, so each root is claimed at its first arrival and an abort or a release starts its count again from zero (#3363). A return landing a trace of such a unit arms is armed primed too, as php hands it a slot of the same unfilled pool (`zend_jit_trace_setup_ret_counter`, `Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:3417-3430`), and a unit the file cache reads back is laid sealed whatever the request's `JIT_G` says, as php's `zend_file_cache_script_load` restores op arrays that never reach `zend_jit_op_array`. The words stay the engine's (ADR-0006), as `docs/divergence.toml`'s `trace:hot-counter-pool` records. A unit a request published with the tier on counts for every later request, as php's shared memory keeps the handlers it was persisted with; one published with it off, the program that called `ini_set` among them, is never traced. `opcache_get_status()['jit']` is the request's `JIT_G` beside the start's buffer, less what the epoch's traces take (`Policy::status`). `opcache.jit_debug` is read where each `TRACE` line is written, as php reads `JIT_G(debug)` there (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:8152-8250`): the request's own bits, `Lines`, at the claim's start line, at an exit taken, at the recording's end and as the recording is settled, for the pipeline's stop, compiled, abort and blacklisted lines, so a request's `ini_set('opcache.jit_debug', ...)` changes the lines it writes from then on. A request whose bits ask for a root's end lines compiles that recording on the request (`Compile::Inline`, §4.8), whatever `jit.compile` says, so the worker writes none. ext/opcache's handler refuses a run-time change to a bit of `ZEND_JIT_DEBUG_PERSISTENT`, as `zend_jit_debug_config` does, so the bits the start alone reads, `PERF` and `PERF_DUMP` for the `PERF` outputs and any bit for the start's lines, are the engine's.

Tested at the seam with no engine: `from_ini` against php's `zend_jit_parse_config_num` cases (`on` is 1254, `function` is 1205, `disable`); the state machine as a table, one row per `(State, Start, Outcome)`, driven over a `Word::default()` through a `Holder::Root` on a fixture `Published` and asserting `read` after each `settle`, including that sixteen aborts take sixteen threshold crossings and blacklist a root, that a side's exit, whose heat runs on through its attempts, is blacklisted by the abort that finds its heat at `opcache.jit_hot_side_exit` plus `opcache.jit_blacklist_side_trace`, its ninth at php's defaults, and that an exit that grows none is blacklisted at the firing that brings its heat there (revision 56), that `claim` returns `None` when the word is not cold, that a `Claim` moved to another thread and dropped there leaves the word `cold` with one more attempt and zero heat; that `read(&Word::sealed())` is `Blacklisted` and `claim` on it is `None`, the layout invariant; dump lines diffed against the oracle.

### 4.8 `compiler`: the pipeline, on its own thread

```rust
pub(crate) struct Job { recorded: Recorded, claim: Claim, name: TraceName }   // name: the place the `TRACE start` line names (revision 34)
pub(crate) struct Compiler { /* under Thread: the engine's tier::Worker and the queue its body serves, Mutex<VecDeque<Job>> with a Condvar */ }
impl Compiler {
    pub(crate) fn new(shared: &Arc<Shared>) -> Compiler;     // a worker under Compile::Thread and none under Mode::Off; its thread starts with the first job
    pub(crate) fn submitter(&self) -> Submitter;             // laid into each request's JitRequest, so a submission reads no engine state (revision 66)
    /// Runs every queued job to completion on the calling thread, and waits for the worker's. Tests.
    pub(crate) fn drain(&self);
}
#[derive(Clone)] pub(crate) struct Submitter(/* the Compiler's worker and queue by Arc, or none: inline */);
impl Submitter {
    /// `Inline`: runs `pipeline` here, its lines added to `lines`, and answers the `Outcome`. `Thread`: enqueues, wakes the worker and answers none.
    pub(crate) fn submit(&self, shared: &Shared, job: Job, lines: &mut Vec<u8>) -> Option<Outcome>;
}
/// `types::build` over the record and its view, then `shared.backend.compile` under the job's name,
/// then `publish` into the table of the epoch the claim holds, then `policy::settle(claim, &outcome)`.
/// Each failure is mapped by one rule, `Abort(stop)` where php recovers and `Blacklist(stop)` where it
/// does not: an off-list `Stop`; an `Abort` from `build` or `compile`, by the stop it carries; a bound's
/// `Full`, `Blacklist(Stop::TooManyTraces)` or its sibling, and `Full::Code`, `Abort(Stop::CompilerError)`
/// (revision 31); a malformed body and a stage that panics, `Abort(Stop::CompilerError)`. The stop and end
/// lines are added to `lines` (revision 34, until #3040 rules).
pub(crate) fn pipeline(shared: &Shared, job: Job, lines: &mut Vec<u8>) -> Outcome;
```

Design it twice on where the pipeline runs:

| | (A) On the request thread, inside the safepoint | **(B) Chosen: one worker per engine, inline on request** | (C) A pool |
|---|---|---|---|
| What the winning request pays | record, build, lower, publish, in its own latency | record, and a queue push | the same as (B) |
| What other requests see | `recording` at the word, `Continue` | the same, for as long as the queue is deep | the same |
| Determinism for tests | complete | complete under `inline`; `force` and `exit-fuzz` imply it | complete under `inline` |
| Memory limits | the compile's allocations count against the request's `memory_limit` | they do not, as php's buffer does not (unverified) | the same |
| Threads per engine | none | one | N, and a scheduler |
| Where a compile panic surfaces | the request boundary | the pipeline's `catch_unwind`, on the worker or inline, reported as php reports a failed compile, `TRACE n abort (JIT compilation error)` where `jit_debug` asks, the claim settled as `CompilerError` | the same |

(B) is chosen. A trace is entered on the next visit to its root in every column, which is php's behaviour, so a request never waits for a compile in any of them; what (A) makes it wait for is its own. (C) is a scheduler for a queue that G7 says holds one job at a time on the kernels; if a request-shaped corpus fills it, the worker count is a `Policy` number and nothing else changes.

What the worker touches. A `Job` is a `Recorded`, which holds the epoch, and a `Claim`, which owns the holder of its word; the worker reaches `Shared` through its own `Arc`. It touches no request, no frame and no map pointer, which is the same discipline compiled code keeps (§E.7); the epoch the view holds keeps the engine's interned strings its units name, so a job outlives the engine that made it (revision 34). A trace compiled for a unit the cache has since evicted is published into the epoch and freed with it, which is correct and costs one compile. The worker is the engine's `tier::Worker`, as ADR-0010's Threads has every thread the engine runs: started through the engine's one home with the first job, parked between jobs, joined before every fork once the job under way is done and woken after it in the process that asked, so a parked worker refuses no `pcntl_fork` and no php-fpm master's fork (revision 34). `Compiler::drop` drops the jobs still in the queue, whose claims settle themselves, and joins the running one; `JitEngine`'s field order puts `compiler` before `shared`, so the worker is gone before what it shares drops. A panic in `publish` cannot poison the table: `publish` is transactional and the lock is taken with `PoisonError::into_inner`.

The dump. `hot` writes the start line and the pipeline the stop and end lines, each to the request it runs on, which is where php's `jit_debug` output goes. A worker could not keep php's order, so a recording whose request's `jit_debug` asks for a root's `stop`, `compiled`, `abort` or `blacklisted` line as it is settled compiles inline, as `force` and `exit-fuzz` do (revision 106), and the oracle diff of `TRACE` lines runs inline under `[regime.jit-inline]`, which sets no `jit.compile` (revision 34; #3040 rules it). A recording that stopped on a failed attempt is settled by the pipeline on the request at once under either setting, as php writes its abort before an inner loop's repeat; only one that compiles is submitted. Under `Compile::Inline`, `submit` is `pipeline` on the calling thread, which is what every per-commit test runs; under `Thread`, `drain` is what a test calls after a request to make the queue empty before asserting a word's state. A request that submitted a trace to the worker claims no word, as while it records, until the trace's word no longer reads recording and, for a loop's trace closed at its header, until the next loop or return site it visits, where php's interpreter would have entered the trace already (`dispatch::Submitted`; revision 55). What `dispatch::quiet` holds is kept as built, as Mitchell ruled on #3191 (revision 71): it keeps (B)'s "a request never waits for a compile", where waiting for a loop's own job would give php's order exactly, but a busy worker makes that wait unbounded, which php's inline compile never is.

Tested at the seam: `pipeline` over a fixture `Recorded` and a `Claim` on a fixture word, asserting the `Outcome` and the word's state for each failure of each stage, including a panicking fixture, with no backend but `dump`; and, natively, a `Thread` test that submits from four threads, drains, and finds every word `compiled` or `blacklisted` and no trace published twice.

### 4.9 `dispatch`: the one machine

```rust
pub(crate) enum Next { Continue, Enter(TraceId), Resume(FrameRef), Bail(Bailout) }

/// The one state machine over a word, for either home. A sealed word reads `Blacklisted` and
/// takes the last arm.
#[inline(always)]
pub(crate) fn visit(req: &mut Request, frame: FrameRef, word: &Word, holder: impl FnOnce() -> Holder, start: Start) -> Next {
    match policy::read(word) {
        State::Compiled(id) => Next::Enter(id),
        State::Cold => {
            let policy = &tier::state::<JitRequest>(req).shared.policy;              // the request's state, no lookup (revision 66)
            if policy::bump(word) >= policy.threshold(start) {
                if record::recording(req) { return Next::Continue }                    // php bumps and does not trace while JIT_G(tracing) (revision 30)
                match policy::claim(holder(), policy, start) { Some(claim) => hot(req, frame, claim), None => Next::Continue }
            } else { Next::Continue }
        }
        State::Recording | State::Blacklisted => Next::Continue,
    }
}

/// The cold path: record, submit, resume where recording stopped. Recording is exclusive per request.
#[inline(never)]
fn hot(req: &mut Request, frame: FrameRef, claim: Claim) -> Next {
    let _recording = RecordingGuard::set(req);                                       // sets the request's exclusion, php's JIT_G(tracing), and clears it on drop: a panic, a bailout or a destroyed fiber's unwind clears it
    match record::run(req, frame, claim.start()) {
        Ok(recorded) => { tier::state::<JitRequest>(req).submitter.submit(Job { recorded, claim }); Next::Resume(req.stack().current()) }
        Err(bailout) => Next::Bail(bailout),          // the claim drops here and settles itself
    }
}

/// From a `Func` word: what `Jit::safepoint` is. Here, and only here, `Continue` means "nothing happened".
/// Outlined: only a request in `Jit`'s loops asks it, under `Bound::On` or past a `Bound::Runtime` hand-off (§4, #3362).
#[inline(never)]
pub(crate) fn safepoint(req: &mut Request, frame: FrameRef, site: Site, held: Held<'_>) -> Flow {
    let state = tier::state::<JitRequest>(req);                                       // one type check, no lookup (§2.2, revision 66)
    if state.shared.policy.mode() == Mode::Off { return Flow::Continue }              // asked under Off all the same, no word is read or written
    let at = site.idx();
    let holder = || Holder::Root { epoch: req.epoch(), func: held.func(), at };       // a hold on the request's epoch, as `Recorded.view`'s (§4.1)
    match visit(req, frame, held.word(req, at), holder, site.into()) {                // the word at the held op array's offset, no frame lookup
        Next::Enter(id) => run(req, frame, id),
        Next::Continue => Flow::Continue,
        Next::Resume(f) => Flow::Resume(f),
        Next::Bail(b) => Flow::Bail(b),
    }
}

/// Trace to trace, until something is not compiled. A trace has run by the time anything is
/// returned, so `Continue` from an exit's visit is `Resume` here.
#[inline(never)]
pub(crate) fn run(req: &mut Request, mut frame: FrameRef, mut id: TraceId) -> Flow {
    let table = &req.engine::<JitEngine>().shared.table;
    let Some(first) = table.get(id) else { return Flow::Continue };                   // not compiled after all (§3.2, §4.5): nothing ran
    let mut trace = first;
    loop {
        let Exited { exit, frame: f } = match enter::enter(req, frame, &trace) { Ok(x) => x, Err(b) => return Flow::Bail(b) };
        frame = f;
        let ex = &trace.exits[exit.0 as usize];
        if let Some(link) = ex.link {
            match table.get(link) { Some(t) => { id = link; trace = t; continue }, None => return Flow::Resume(frame) }   // the window §3.2 names
        }
        let holder = || Holder::Exit { epoch: req.epoch(), trace: id, exit };
        match visit(req, frame, &ex.word, holder, Start::Side { parent: id, exit }) {
            Next::Enter(side) => match table.get(side) { Some(t) => { id = side; trace = t }, None => return Flow::Resume(frame) },
            Next::Continue => return Flow::Resume(frame),
            Next::Resume(f) => return Flow::Resume(f),
            Next::Bail(b) => return Flow::Bail(b),
        }
    }
}
```

**The exclusion is the request's** (revision 30, #2999). php excludes a second recording with `JIT_G(tracing)`, one flag per thread that `zend_jit_trace_hot_root` tests and sets around the recorder (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:8140`, `:8170-8173`) and that no fiber switch saves; `EG(jit_trace_num)`, which `zend_fiber_vm_state` does save, numbers the trace executing and excludes nothing. Here the exclusion is request state outside the switch, set by the recording guard and cleared when the guard drops: on the recording's return, on a panic, or on the unwind that destroys a fiber suspended inside it (design §5.11). A resumer therefore records nothing while a fiber's recording is suspended, and a fiber started inside a recording records no root of its own, as php's do; a fiber suspended inside a recording and kept alive holds the exclusion until it is destroyed or the request ends, which is php's behaviour as well.

`visit` is the machine for both homes; the only thing a home contributes is the `Holder` it would give a claim, and it is a closure so that the compiled and the cold-below-threshold paths, which are every visit but one, take no hold on the epoch. A sealed word never crosses a threshold, so a root holder is never built for a request-scoped op array, whose unit no epoch holds; the invariant is written once, in `policy` (§4.7). The exclusion is read at an activation alone, before its word, one load of the request's state beside the engine's, so it costs nothing at a loop header or a return and nothing under `Off`; php reads `JIT_G(tracing)` only after a crossing (`php-src-jit-hot-root.txt:30-33`), a difference no program observes (revision 30). `hot` never touches the word: the claim holds it. Recording ran the program forward, so `hot` resumes wherever recording stopped, and a `Threw` stop resumes in the frame `HANDLE_EXCEPTION` runs in (§4.1). The compiled trace is entered on the next visit to the root, as in php: a visit that finds a word compiled hands its id to `run` (revision 36). `run` follows links without reading a word, because a link is a build-time fact on the exit, enters side traces by the id in the exit's word, and borrows each trace from the table, whose slots are written once and live as long as the epoch the request holds (§4.5), so entering a trace takes no hold of its own; the one hold `dispatch` takes is a holder's, on the claim path only. The three meanings of "not compiled" are stated on the three places: at a root whose slot is empty nothing ran and `Continue` is right; after an exit a trace did run, so a missing link or side target is `Resume` of the landed frame; and a word that reads `Recording` or `Blacklisted` at an exit is `Resume` for the same reason. Two exits are not side exits and `run` visits neither word: one with a link, which it follows, and a trace's `leaves`, the exit a trace that does not loop ends through, php's `zend_jit_trace_return` to the interpreter; at any other exit that enters nothing, `run` writes php's `TRACE n exit m` line where `opcache.jit_debug` asks for it, as `zend_jit_trace_exit` writes it unless the interrupt is raised or the request records. An exit's word is bumped toward `opcache.jit_hot_side_exit` and, past it, claimed under `Holder::Exit` for a side trace recorded from the exit's landing with what the exit knew of its frame's slots, php's stack map, as its entry types, published into the same id space and entered by the id the word then holds; an exit that names no opline, a table's jump or a call that may be another's, which leaves the frame where it stood, is claimed as any other, and its side trace, recorded from where the frame stands, holds that opline at its first op, php's IP guard (`zend_jit_trace_opline_guard`), compiled code comparing it with where `enter` found the frame, through an exit that names none and grows a side of its own (revision 67); one that grows none, php's `ZEND_JIT_EXIT_TO_VM`, is claimed by nothing and blacklisted at the firing that brings its heat to `opcache.jit_hot_side_exit` plus `opcache.jit_blacklist_side_trace`, writing php's `---- EXIT m/k blacklisted` and no line after (revision 56), and a recording that stops at a compiled trace's root enters it there, as php's interpreter goes on at that opline, whose handler is the trace (revision 52). As built (#3229's ruling, revision 81; #4108's ruling, revision 142), a link and an exit whose side trace is published are jumps in the trace's own code (§4.3, §4.6), and `run` meets a linked exit or a published side only where the request's door is closed: the first crossing of each into its trace in a request, which opens it, and every exit that grows none. `run` goes on from the trace that answered, which a jump may have entered.

A return root is php's: the caller's opline past the call (`ZEND_JIT_TRACE_START_RETURN` on `opline + 1`), whose counter is armed only where a compiled trace returns into a caller that has no trace of its own (`zend_jit_trace_setup_ret_counter`, `Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:3404-3418`). `Site::Return`, the callee's `RETURN`, bumps and claims nothing (#3036). So a return's site visits no word of the callee's: `dispatch::safepoint` reads the landing past the call its caller stands at, which reads sealed, and is left, until a compiled trace armed it; one compiled is entered and one hot is claimed, under `Holder::Landing`, in the caller at the landing, since the site is asked past the pop, as php's interpreter has returned before the landing's handler runs (revision 141). Where the landing is a loop's root, which no trace arms, the return reads that loop's word instead, as php's handler there is the loop's, its heat or the trace that replaced it: a compiled loop is entered there, as it is where a trace or a recording leaves its frame there, and a cold one is left uncounted, its heat its back edge's, one count a turn as php's header counts each arrival once; one rule says which word an opline's arrival reads (`dispatch::goes_on`), an armed landing's first, and it takes no site at the condition, the return's own site serving (revision 91, #3318). What a trace arms is `record::landings`, php's calls of `zend_jit_trace_setup_ret_counter` as its code generator makes them, the opline past each call into the root's op array for a recursive call and, for a trace that hands on to another trace or to the interpreter, past its last opline where that is a call, an `include` or `eval` or a `yield`, and past the call each frame below the one it ends in made; the compiler arms them when the trace is published, where `opcache.jit_hot_return` is not zero. A trace or a recording that leaves its frame at an armed landing visits it, as php's interpreter runs the handler of the opline it goes on at, except a trace's own root, where php's `zend_jit_trace_exit` runs the opline's own handler, and a recording that stops at a call's activation visits that entry's word, a trace just published at a `recursive call` stop, which stands at its own root, entered there as a loop's is (revision 62), and a loop's recording that closes at its header and whose trace fails to compile visits the header's word there, as php's interpreter goes on through the header's counter handler, which an abort that may recover leaves in place: a claim records again from the header, and where none is made the condition is stepped to where it goes, so the back edge does not count the same arrival twice (`dispatch::counted`, revision 119); and a `RETURN` php compiles with `zend_jit_return`, past a trace's lowest frame with no opline recorded after it, is held to the opline the trace links at or goes back to, php's IP guard in `zend_jit_leave_func` (revision 61), which takes no exit where it fails: the trace escapes, as php's `jit_stub_trace_escape` does, with no exit line and no word, and the interpreter goes on through the handler of the opline the return left the frame at, the landing's trace where one is compiled there (`lower::ESCAPED`, `enter::Left::Escaped`; revision 64). A return php hands to its handler there, a `RETURN_BY_REF`, or a `RETURN` of a file's own code or of an operand that may be undefined (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_trace.c:5580-5595`), escapes nowhere, whether this tier steps it or leaves its frame in place: it is held to the same opline by an exit within its line, numbered where php's `zend_jit_trace_handler` takes its exit point past the handler (`Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_ir.c:17200-17330`), which names no opline, stores nothing, knows no type of the caller it lands in, and grows a side trace where the interpreter stands (revision 127, #3688). Where a trace escapes, its code reads the word of the landing it escaped to, as php's escape stub runs the handler of the opline the frame stands at (zend_jit_trace_escape_stub, Z/ext/opcache/jit/zend_jit_ir.c:2488-2497), which the root's publish wrote (zend_jit_trace.c:7595). A trace's escape reads its door in the request, which names the landing the request escaped to from that escape and the trace compiled at it. Where the frame stands at that landing, the trace is entered through the door. Anywhere else, or where the door is closed, the escape reads the landing's word as before: a word that reads compiled is entered through its trace's entrance in the table, and any other answers `ESCAPED`, the crossing visiting the landing and opening the door where it is the request's first escape from there. No published trace keeps state of the landings it escapes to. (Revision 112, #3299's ruling; revision 142, #4108's.)

Tested at the seam: `visit` as a table over `(State, Start)` on a `Word::default()` and a `Word::sealed()` with a fixture engine, asserting `Next`; `visit` with the request's exclusion set at a threshold crossing, asserting `Continue` and a bumped word; a fiber suspended inside a recording and never resumed, asserting no root recorded on the resumer until the fiber is destroyed, and one after (revision 30); a witness whose destructor runs a hot loop inside a recording, asserting one `TRACE start` line, a second root recorded on the next request, and the exclusion clear after a panicking fixture recording; `run` with two hand-assembled traces, one linking to the other and one exiting to a cold word, asserting the sequence of entries through a `Probe`-style counter kernel and that the return is `Resume` of the landed frame; and, under `force`, that every witness enters through `safepoint` and returns through `run` with the oracle's output.

---

## 5. The kernel face: where the `extern "C"` wrappers live

### 5.1 Design it twice

| | (A) In `php_engine`, by macro beside each kernel (§E.8) | **(B) In `php_jit::enter`, by one `kernels!` list (chosen)** |
|---|---|---|
| Can it exist there | only with reference parameters, since dereferencing `*mut Request` is `unsafe` and `php_engine` forbids it; the soundness obligation then rests on the caller and is documented nowhere in the type | yes; the two raw-pointer dereferences are in the crate that owns the calling convention |
| `php_engine`'s public surface | grows by one `extern "C"` item per kernel, roughly three hundred, for exactly one caller | grows by zero items; the kernels themselves become visible to `php_jit` under `tier.lock` (§5.3) |
| Knowledge of the JIT's convention (`Status`, `opline` as index, out-pointer) | inside `php_engine` | inside `php_jit` |
| Drift between the list and the kernels | none; the wrapper is generated at the definition | a signature change in `ops` fails `php_jit`'s build, because the wrapper calls the kernel; no checker |
| Deletion test | delete the macro and the wrappers reappear in `php_jit` once, by macro: nothing spreads | delete the list and each wrapper reappears at its call site in `lower`, three hundred times: it earns its keep |

(B) is chosen. The wrappers are not `php_engine`'s interface; they are `php_jit`'s interface to its own compiled code, an internal seam, and the price of keeping them there is a hand-written list the compiler checks. The review's proposal to generate the list from `ops.toml` is not taken here and is left for a later revision.

### 5.2 One `step`, not two hundred opcode wrappers

php's `zend_jit_trace_handler` calls the opline's handler (§A.9); here the handler is an arm of the generated `match`, and `step` is that `match` run once. A trace that meets an opline `lower` does not know stores `frame.opline` and calls `php_jit_step`. One item where two hundred would have been generated.

### 5.3 The tier face, and `tier.lock`

`php_jit` sees more of `php_engine` than `api`; it is the only crate that does, `php_macros` depending on no workspace crate (ADR-0007). What it sees is listed and locked, by principle 8: `tier.lock` has two sections. The **face**: `Tier`, `Site`, `Flow`, `Nop`, `execute`, `step`, `Stepped`, `Word` with `halves` and `sealed`, `SafepointIdx`, `FuncRef` with `func` and `word`, `Epoch` with `epoch` and `Epoch::word`, the tier's epoch slot `epoch_state` and `Epoch::tier_state` (revision 27), `ClassId`, `MapPtrRaw`, `Limits::interrupt`, `TraceNum` with `NONE`, `from_raw` and `raw`, `trace_num` and `set_trace_num`, `OplineIdx`, `Request::map_base`, `Request::stack`, the `api` items a tier's state needs (`Extension`, `ExtensionBuilder`, `Refusal`, `Start`, `StartupIni`, `Request::engine`, `Request::state`, `DiagnosticsSink`), the compile worker's `Parked` and `Worker`, which the engine starts and a fork joins (revision 34), and `UnitView` with its builders: a few dozen items, reviewed one by one. The face also holds what the crossing reaches (revision 32): `room` and `at_room`, a frame by its room, php's `execute_data`, and back; `current`, the frame the loop goes on in; `stand`, `SAVE_OPLINE()`; `primed`, the callee an `INIT_*`'s cache slot holds; `free_operands`, an exit's `FREE_OP1` and `FREE_OP2`; and `Diagnostics::raised`. The **kernels**: the engine items the `kernels!` list's lines name after their `=` that the face does not list already, generated into the lock from the list itself by `api-check` and diffed as one section, never kept from the committed file, so a kernel's engine item changing is a lock diff that names it and nothing else; with `step` on the face and `copy` served by `php_heap` alone, that is `tier::release` today (revision 32). `xtask api-check` allows `php_jit` to import from `php_engine` only what either section names.

```rust
/// What a trace may read of the op arrays, literals and engine-immutable classes it touched. Built
/// by the recorder from the request; holds the request's epoch and the ids it accepted; `Send`.
pub struct UnitView { /* private */ }
impl UnitView {
    pub fn new(req: &Request) -> UnitView;                                    // holds req's epoch
    pub fn hold(&mut self, req: &Request, f: FuncRef) -> bool;                // false, and holds nothing, for a request-scoped op array
    pub fn hold_class(&mut self, req: &Request, c: ClassId) -> bool;          // false, and holds nothing, unless the ClassId is published
    pub fn op_array(&self, f: FuncRef) -> Option<&OpArray>;                   // none unless hold accepted it
    pub fn is_main(&self, f: FuncRef) -> Option<bool>;                        // f is its file's own code, php's !function_name (revision 26)
    pub fn parameters(&self, f: FuncRef) -> Option<&[Parameter]>;             // its declaration's parameters, php's arg_info (revision 26)
    pub fn class(&self, c: ClassId) -> Option<&LinkedClass<ClassId>>;         // none unless hold_class accepted it
    pub fn cache_slot(&self, f: FuncRef, slot: CacheSlot) -> Option<MapPtrRaw>; // the engine's offset of f's cache and the slot in it
    pub fn hold_place(&mut self, req: &Request, frame: FrameRef, at: OplineIdx, c: ClassId) -> bool; // the declared slot the cache names (revision 93)
    pub fn place(&self, f: FuncRef, at: OplineIdx, c: ClassId) -> Option<PropertySlot>;       // none unless hold_place took it
}
```

`hold` accepts `f` where the view's epoch keeps a row at its offset, which only a held unit's op array has, so a request-scoped one is refused by its offset's kind. `cache_slot` takes the slot, since which field of an opline holds it is the opcode's to say, read where the tier lowers the opline. `UnitView: Send` and `Epoch: Send + Sync` are asserted at compile time beside the types (revision 24).

**The frame's layout** (#3222's ruling, revision 79). The face holds `FRAME`, one `FrameLayout` value that says where a frame's header words stand in its room, php's `execute_data` (`opline`, `info`, `num_args`, `caller`, `returns_to`, `cache`, `annex`, `this`, `closure`), the bits a leave in place refuses by (`leaves_by_kernel`, `caller_loop`) and what a caller and a return slot are numbered from, and where the request's VM stack keeps its top page's top and end, php's `EG(vm_stack_top)` and `EG(vm_stack_end)`, its frame table and a record's fields, and the innermost room's address. Every offset is `offset_of!` over `exec::Header` and `php_heap::VmStack::LAYOUT`, computed in the engine's build, and every accessor is a `tier.lock` item, so a field or a bit added, removed or renamed is a diff of the lock and a moved offset is not; no offset is written in `lower` or in a lock. Beside it the face holds what a call in place needs of the request: `PlainCall` and its `Activation`, the call a recording takes of a push `push_call` laid down plain (`plain_call`, `UnitView::hold_call`, `UnitView::call`), with the flags, entry opline and cache its activation writes where it serves no prologue; `call_image`, the header the request lays out for that call, which `enter` keeps per request and per trace; `stack`, the address of the request's VM stack; `laid`, whether a callee's cache is laid down; and `UnitView::observed`, an observer declared, under which nothing is made in place.

`heap.lock` holds `VmStack::entry`, `push_entry`, `current`, `MapTable::base`, and `FrameRef::func() -> FuncRef` as a frame-header field, and for the frame's layout `VmStack::LAYOUT` with `StackLayout` and `FramesLayout`, and `HeaderImage`, a header's bytes as a push lays them, which code that pushes the same call in place copies. For the variables a leave in place frees it holds `Zval::GC_INFO_AT` and `Zval::LEAK_BITS`, where a counted block keeps php's `GC_TYPE_INFO` and the bits of it of which a value `gc_possible_root` may take has none, `GC_MAY_LEAK`'s mask, the one place the heap's own test reads it too (revision 89).

---

## 6. Dependency direction and crate placement

```
php_heap ◄── php_unit ◄── php_compile ◄── php_engine ──► php_macros ◄── php_ext
   ▲            ▲                             ▲   ▲
   │            │                             │   └── php_ext ◄── ext/* ◄── php_bundle ──► (feature jit) php_jit
   └────────────┴──────── php_jit ────────────┘         ▲            ▲
                          (tier.lock face of php_engine; heap.lock; unit types;         │
                           cranelift-codegen, cranelift-frontend, cranelift-native,     │
                           regalloc2, rustix or memmap2)                                │
                                                        php ──► php_bundle ──► php_sapi ──► php_proto
                                                        xtask (in-process phpt, witness, bench) ──► php_bundle
```

Rules, all enforced by `xtask api-check`. `php_jit` depends on `php_engine`, `php_heap`, `php_unit`, the Cranelift crates, `rustix` (or `memmap2`) for `memfd_create`, the sealing `fcntl` and the two `mmap`s on Linux; it imports from `php_engine` only `tier.lock` items. `php_engine` never depends on `php_jit`. `php_bundle` depends on `ext/*`, on `php_ext` for the `ExtensionBuilder` type its list returns, and, under the `jit` feature on the four targets, on `php_jit` for one alias; it names `php_engine::tier::Nop` through `php_ext`'s re-export otherwise:

```rust
// php_bundle
#[cfg(all(feature = "jit", any(target_os = "linux", target_os = "macos"), any(target_arch = "x86_64", target_arch = "aarch64")))]
pub type Tier = php_jit::Jit;
#[cfg(not(all(feature = "jit", any(target_os = "linux", target_os = "macos"), any(target_arch = "x86_64", target_arch = "aarch64"))))]
pub type Tier = php_ext::tier::Nop;
pub fn enabled() -> &'static [ExtensionBuilder];   // ext/* only; the tier is not in this list

// every host, the same line
let engine = Engine::builder().extensions(php_bundle::enabled()).tier::<php_bundle::Tier>().build()?;
```

Design-it-twice on the chooser: the `php` binary could make the choice, but then `xtask`'s in-process phpt host, the embed host and the dev server each repeat it, and design §4.6's deletion test for `php_bundle` applies to the tier verbatim. The bundle is the one place the feature flags live, so the tier is a feature flag there. The Cranelift crate set and `rustix` or `memmap2` are counted against `[budget] lock_packages` on day 31 (§F.4, design §11 row 31); `cranelift-jit` is not used, because its memory provider leaks by default and knows nothing of `MAP_JIT` (§B.8).

`php_unit` carries `OpArray.safepoints` and the compiler writes safepoint numbers into the field `ops.toml` names; both are unit-format items under ADR-0001 and `unit.lock` names them. `php_engine` names `Published`, `Word::sealed` and `Limits::interrupt` on the tier face. `php_heap` carries the entry slot and `current` on `VmStack`.

---

## 7. Testability through the interfaces

| Module | Seam | Test adapter or fixture | Oracle | Mode |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `Tier` in `php_engine` | `execute::<T>`, `EngineBuilder::tier` | `Probe` adapter asserting `(site, number)` sequences on witness scripts: `(Entry, 0)` exactly once per call and never for a call whose `RECV` throws; `(Loop, n)` with one `n` for two back-edges to one header, on `for`, `foreach` with `continue`, and `do { } while (!$x)`; `(Return, n)` never for the entry frame; and that its request state exists | none needed | `cargo test -p php_engine` |
| `step` in `php_engine` | `step` | a witness single-stepped to completion produces the output of `execute::<Nop>`; one witness per `Stepped` arm; `Pushed` lands at the first body opline with `Entry` raised; a fused compare-and-branch is one step; `Done` at the entry frame's return, with and without a pending exception | `Capture` output | `cargo test -p php_engine` |
| the word in `php_engine` | `tier::word`, `tier::func`, `Epoch::word`, `OpArray.safepoints` | every backward jump and return in a witness carries the number of a word; two back-edges to one header carry one number; the entry is zero; a held unit's words are zero and one word for every request of the epoch; an `eval` op array's words are sealed and `UnitView::hold` refuses it | `compile-identity` unchanged | `cargo test -p php_engine` |
| the invariant in `php_compile` | `compile` | every op array of the per-commit corpus has a header for every backward jump and a column for every backward jump opcode | none: an assertion | `cargo test -p php_compile`; `compile-identity` sweep |
| `VmStack` entry slot in `php_heap` | `entry`, `push_entry`, `current` | nested `execute` from a native, a bailout inside the nested one, a fiber switch between two nestings, under Miri | none needed | `cargo test -p php_heap` |
| `record` | `run`, `dump`/`parse` | `Capture` host, `jit.mode=force`; recorded traces as fixtures; one fixture per `Stepped` arm, including a `Return` start, an `Interpreter` stop at a call into an `eval` function that arms the caller's landing past it, a `Return` stop at a return into an `eval`'s caller, and a fused branch each way | oracle's `TRACE_START|TRACE_STOP` lines through `oracle::compare` | per commit on the seven kernels |
| `types` | `build` | parsed fixtures in, `lower::dump::render` out, golden diff; one fixture per clobbering kernel class | goldens fixed once against `TRACE_TSSA` | per commit, each program served once for the view its record is read through (revision 26) |
| `lower` | `Backend::new`, `Backend::compile` | CLIF text golden per fixture, the interrupt test in every loop golden that takes one; bytes run under `force`; `Unsupported` on a stubbed host | oracle output | per commit |
| `table` | `publish`, `get` | `HeapPages` under Miri: bounds, segments, `Full`, a full table that stops compiling, a new epoch's table empty, a panicking publish, chunk return; `mov; ret` through two views natively; the concurrent publish test natively on both targets; one `PERF` line | php's `perf` map line | per commit |
| `enter` | `enter`, `kernels!` | hand-assembled body calling `php_jit_step` through an `ExitCtx`; a panicking test kernel; the nested-entry panic; every wrapper under `force`; `jit.mode=exit-fuzz` for every guard it fails (G5); `set_time_limit(1); for (;;) {}` and a `pcntl` signal into a compiled loop under `force`; a fiber suspended from inside a kernel a trace called | oracle output, byte for byte | per commit on witnesses, nightly on `Zend/tests` and `tests/` |
| `policy` | `from_ini`, `read`/`bump`/`claim`/`settle`, `dump` | php's config-number cases; the state table over a `Word::default()` and a `Word::sealed()` through a `Holder`; a `Claim` dropped on another thread; the heat reset on `Abort`; dump diff | oracle's `ini_get` and `jit_debug` output | per commit, no engine |
| `compiler` | `pipeline`, `submit`, `drain` | a fixture `Recorded` and `Claim`, asserting `Outcome` and word state for each failure of each stage, including a panicking fixture; four submitting threads and a drain | none | per commit |
| `dispatch` | `visit`, `safepoint`, `run` | `visit` as a table over `(State, Start)` and over the request's exclusion; the nested-recording witness; `Off` touching no word under a `Probe`-style word spy; two hand-assembled traces, one linking and one exiting cold, asserting `Resume` of the landed frame | none | per commit |
| whole tier | `Jit` | G1 to G7 under the protocol (§E.11), with `jit.compile` in both settings for G6 and G7 | oracle with `opcache.jit=tracing` and `off` | per commit for G1, G4, G5; run of record for the flip |

Every row tests at an interface named in §4; none reaches past one. `force` and `exit-fuzz` are how the whole tier is tested through `Jit` alone: `force` compiles every eligible root at first execution so the witnesses and phpt corpora exercise every wrapper and guard predicate, `exit-fuzz` exercises the deopt protocol, as ruled on #3055 (revision 40): Under `jit.mode=exit-fuzz` every guard whose exit is right whatever its value holds fails once per process, the interrupt guard included; the interpreter continues at the landing, and the run must match the oracle byte for byte. A jump's direction guard and `push_call`'s refusal are not failed: their exits are right only where their condition holds (#3055). Zero divergence under both is G5, and G5 blocks the default.

---

## 8. Deepening moves, and the shallow modules they replace

| Risk | Why shallow | Move |
|---|---|---|
| A `Mode` trait beside `Tier`; five tier hooks; a `Recording` adapter and a third instantiation of the loop | two type parameters, four instantiations, one meaningless; "enter" is not a request the loop makes; a hook and a case two of three adapters implement as nothing | one trait, one hook; `Site` is a folded constant; enter is a branch; the recorder is a loop over `step` (§2) |
